- The Truth About Shopping Addiction (And Why It’s Never About Stuff): Kind: captions Language: en The truth about shopping addiction. Shopping addiction doesn't feel like a problem at first. Feels like reward, a little hit of excitement, a treat after a hard day. You tell yourself it's fine. You're just browsing. But what starts as comfort becomes a coping mechanism. And soon it's not about what you're buying. It's about what you're avoiding. But the real cost isn't money. is what's happening underneath. People think shopping addiction is about greed. It's not. It's about relief. You are chasing a moment, the anticipation, the click. Package arriving feels like control. It feels like escape. But it it never lasts because it was never about the item. It was about part of you that need to feel okay for 5 minutes. Shopping addiction follows a pattern. stress, scroll, spend, shame, repeat. Every purchase gives a dopamine spike. And then you crash, guilt, anxiety, financial panic. You swear it's the last time, but the next wave of discomfort hits and the loop starts again. And breaking it means seeing what it's really feeding. Shopping can be a mask for loneliness, for fear, for not feeling good enough. Every purchase becomes a way to rewrite the story. If I buy this, maybe I'll finally feel complete. But no item can fill an emotional void. That's not failure. That's being human. But there's another way to feel whole. You know, people say just stop spending. Like it's that simple. But if you take away the shopping without replacing the need it fills, the pain doesn't go away. the void just moves somewhere else. Budgeting won't fix emotional hunger because this isn't a math problem. It's a coping strategy and it needs to be treated like one. Healing from shopping addiction isn't about deprivation. It's about reconnection. What are you really trying to soothe? What would it look like to feel okay without needing to spend to get there? small changes, micro pauses, learning to sit with the urge instead of obeying it. That's where the reset begins. This isn't about vanity or weakness. It's about pain. Shopping addiction is one of the most hidden, least understood struggles because it's so easy to hide behind success, style, or status. But behind the bags and boxes is someone who just wants to feel okay. And the good news, there's nothing wrong with you. You just need help learning a different way. If this sounds familiar, you know how to look. Shopping addiction is real and it's treatable. If you're ready to take the first steps, call us at 844-5943. What's one thing you thought would fix everything but didn't? Subscribe for more real talk. Zero judgment. Your piece is worth more than anything you could buy.

- How to Stop Shopping Addiction Signs, Diagnosis & Real-Life Impact: Kind: captions Language: en Compulsive shopping is on the rise, but is it a real addiction? We unpack the science, the symptoms, and the controversy behind this misunderstood disorder. More people are asking, is shopping addiction a real mental health disorder or just bad habits with a credit card? Researchers still cannot agree on the answer, and that disagreement has big consequences. So let's start with the basics. What exactly is shopping addiction? Well, clinically, it's often called a compulsive buying disorder. It's not about the occasional online splurge or a Boxing Day blowout. We're talking about repeated irresistible urges to shop that cause distress, disrupt daily life, and often spirals into debt, shame and broken relationships. Back in the early 1900s, German psychiatrists Kraeplin and Bleuler described this as oniomania, a form of impulsive insanity. They grouped it with kleptomania and pyromania. So this idea has been around for a long time, but it is struggling to find a place in modern psychiatry. So is shopping addiction officially recognized? It is not in the DSM 5, the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, which guides psychiatric diagnoses in the USA and also in much of the rest of the world. The American Psychiatric association says there's not enough consistent research to support it. However, the World Health Organization's ICD 11 takes a slightly different stance. It includes compulsive buying shopping disorder under the category of other specified impulse control disorders. That is a step forward, but still is not full recognition as a standalone disorder. This matters because classification affects diagnosis, insurance coverage, and also treatment pathways. So why the hesitation? Some experts argue the term shopping addiction medicalizes a moral or lifestyle problem. Is it really a disorder, or is it just irresponsible spending? Others say the problem is very real but hasn't been studied enough, or that it overlaps with other diagnoses like obsessive compulsive disorder, anxiety or mood disorders. That overlap makes it hard to isolate shopping addiction as its own thing. And there's concern about opening the floodgates. If shopping counts, then what about gaming? What about social media? What about binge watching tv? Where do we draw the line between addictive and excessive? So how do you diagnose someone with compulsive shopping? Psychiatrists often use criteria proposed by McElroy and colleagues. You'll hear a lot of familiar features. Intrusive thoughts, irresistible urges, repeated purchases beyond budget or need, and significant distress. Importantly, it cannot be explained by another mental health condition like mania. There are also screening tools like the Compulsive Buying Scale and The Bergen Shopping Addiction Scale. These look at things like craving, mood changes, guilt and loss of control. Very similar to how we screen for gambling or substance use disorders. So what does the data say? How common is it? Estimates vary depending on how you measure it. Meta analyses suggest around 5% of the general population may meet the criteria for shopping addiction. Among university students and regular shoppers, the rates go up to 8% or even 16%. Most studies show a strong gender imbalance. Around 80 to 90% of identified compulsive shoppers are women. Some researchers think that that's a real difference. Others argue that men under report or express it differently, like calling it collecting instead of shopping. The typical age of onset is the late teens or early 20s. That's often when people gain financial independence and get access to credit for the first time. What does it actually look like then in real life? Well, the cycle often follows four anticipation, preparation, shopping and spending. There's a buildup of tension followed by a release or high when the purchase happens. But that relief is short lived. What follows is guilt, regret and sometimes a need to hide the purchases from family or partners. Over time, the spending gets riskier. People may go into debt, borrow from friends or lie to cover up their behavior. Some even resort to crimes like theft or fraud. Just like with other addictions, there's often a clear impact on mental health, relationships and work life. And yet it's still not in the dsm. So what now? More researchers are calling for official recognition, especially as online shopping makes it easier to hide the behaviour and harder to stop. Instant gratification, targeted marketing and 247 access all feed the cycle, including shopping, addiction and diagnostic manuals could help people get proper treatment and reduce stigma. But until then, it's often overlooked or misdiagnosed. So here's the real question. If a repeated behavior causes distress, is difficult to control and causes harm, does it really matter whether it's in a DSM manual? That's all for today, folks. I'm Dr. Fergal Armstrong and this has been cracking addiction.

- The Normalization of Shopping Addictions: Kind: captions Language: en We've entered an era where being addicted to shopping is seen as endearing. You can boast about blacking out and going on a shopping spree. >> So, um, I blacked out kind of bad. >> You can joke about how your shelves are literally bending because they're overflowing with products. >> Hi, I'm Laney, also known as the overconumption girl, and today we're going to be showing you every single product that I have. Oh my god, my hat's getting ruined. Look at the hole. >> You could have a whole photo shoot with your beaded bag collection. I don't GIVE A I'M still in the house. I'm still in a house. I don't give a Do I look like I give a Because I don't. >> And it's seen as cute and aspirational even. I was scrolling through Instagram last night where I came across a video from BBC that was interviewing a woman about her shopping addiction. This was the video. >> It was the first thing I would think about when I woke up. There would be times where I could hit 14 hours a day screen time quite easily. You keep looking and looking and looking in the same way someone might keep drinking and drinking and drinking because they haven't quite reached the point of escapism that they were hoping to reach. This is the main room in my flat where I keep everything. I would say there's probably over 500 items in here. The biggest collection I've got is jackets. I think there's about 80. Probably got at least 100 pairs of footwear. It's like a physical and an emotional drowning because you don't know how to stop it. That recognition of it being an addiction and it being applicable to me was yeah was a bit of a penny drop moment. I do think the shopping addiction needs to be taken more seriously. Just the sort of surface level what everybody else is seeing. You're just buying clothes and you're not causing yourself any harm. Yeah. It was just not noticed in the same way that I think someone would notice someone drinking too much or or using drugs. And you know what the top comment was to this video? God forbid a girl has hobbies. The cavalier way that we've normalized over consumption, treating it like a joke or even a badge of honor, it is deeply problematic. It's like spending your weekend splurging at Sephora or upgrading your phone every year or buying yourself stuff to cure your depression is just another relatable millennial or Gen Z experience. Now, let me be clear. Having a shopping addiction does not make you a bad person. I think it should be handled with the same level of care and empathy as any other addiction. And surely our collective shop till you drop mindset is not born out of our own individual weakness. It is very much baked into the culture that we live in. But that being said, shrugging off a compulsive need to consume as just cutesy and quirky is dangerous because it trivializes the very real consequences that come with chronic overconumption. Let's talk about how we've normalized shopping addictions. [music] >> [music] >> In America, having a shopping addiction is our default factory setting. Not in that we are inherently born with a shopping addiction, but in that we are taught to have one. Think about it. As children, we're inundated with toys. Our parents and relatives are showering us forever with the latest gadgets every birthday and every Christmas. You had a PlayStation 2 that was perfectly functioning, but now the PlayStation 3 came out and you have to have the latest and greatest gadget. So now you're putting that on your Christmas list that year. Entire marketing departments devote themselves to capturing kids attention and loyalty early, knowing that if you hook someone on wanting to overconume things at age 5, they're going to be a customer for life. Now, to be clear, there's nothing inherently wrong with buying kids toys. Play is how we learn. It's how kids develop their perception of colors and words and language. Like, obviously, there is a pedagogical value to toys. And beyond that, there's an entertainment value that is very necessary for a child's developing mind. The problem, though, is that play has been overwhelmingly overtaken by profit. What used to be about curiosity and creativity is now a race for the newest, flashiest, and most unboxing worthy item. And when you're a teenager, at least for those of us who grew up in a time where malls were still very ubiquitous, the mall was the social hub. In her 2019 book on fire, the case for the Green New Deal, author and activist Naomi Klein, who as you know if you've been watching for a while, we enjoy on this channel. In her book, she wrote, quote, "The problem is not human nature as we've often been told. We weren't born having to shop this much, and we have, in our recent past, been just as happy, in many cases happier, consuming significantly less. The problem is the inflated role that consumption has come to play in our particular era. Late capitalism teaches us to create ourselves through our consumer choices. Shopping is how we form our identities, find community, and express ourselves. Thus, telling people that they can't shop as much as they want because the planet support systems are overburdened can be understood as a kind of attack akin to telling them that they cannot truly be themselves. As the chapter of this book is aptly titled, "Being consumers is all we know." But I know firsthand how not funny, how not cute the consequences of our overconumption can be. Overspending is very, very easy. Paying back the credit card debt that you shopped with though after the fact is actually hard. America makes it very easy to spend money but very hard to make money. But again, paying off that bill for that lipstick that you just bought, that can be an uphill battle. But we don't need to allow oppressive systems that were not built to protect our humanity, that were built in constant extraction and policing to allow us to become the oppressors of our own bodies to a point of compulsion. Compulsive shopping known clinically as compulsive buying disorder is very real. Even though there's this stereotype of the bubbly shopaholic, oblivious to everything but shopping, this is often a response, a trauma response to stress, to anxiety, to mental health struggles. But mental health care is stigmatized and it's unaffordable. Because we live in such an abbleist society, people are rightfully afraid to be labeled. They are afraid to seek diagnosis, to seek treatment. You're expected to keep your pain private, to hold yourself together, to perform functionality. Even when you're breaking down, it doesn't matter what's going on in the world or in your personal life. You're just supposed to keep showing up for work and acting like everything is fine. Many of us don't even realize that we're unwell. We've been conditioned to normalize symptoms of a very sick society. We tell ourselves that everything is fine as long as we can afford the small distractions that can numb us from our everyday lives. We think that because we're surviving that means that we're stable. That because we're numb that we're at peace. And so we self soothe with the easiest addictions that the system provides for us. Anything that keeps us distracted from the fact that we're hurting. And it's okay to embrace that because that means that you're a human being. And I will never say that getting away from your problems temporarily is not good for you. But emphasis on temporarily. A lot of mental unwwellness or lack of mental hygiene is treated as something that we should hide. I know in many black communities, there's this added layer of stigma. If you go to therapy, then you're airing your business or you're betraying family privacy. We get this message that we always have to push through it and the only solution is praying on it and that's all we can do. And we should never let outsiders know that we're struggling. And so we learn to internalize it. And then we self-medicate with whatever is easiest to access from the market, which is often a shopping hall, a new outfit, a new car. Often times, instead of facing our pain headon, we buy our way around it. Because consumption is the most socially acceptable way of coping. Welladjusted, happy, fulfilled people are antithetical to capitalism. Because if people were actually happy and physically healthy and emotionally healthy, they wouldn't have to numb themselves at all. People are also working longer hours with less stability, all while being told that if they work harder, then that's all it will take and that this is an individual failing, that they're not working hard enough. Add to that the disintegration of community. I've talked about that a lot. The ways in which we've lost real connection. I mean, even right now, I'm talking to you through the camera. I wish that we could have a face tof face conversation where we can go back and forth, but there's this circularity in the way that we communicate. All of this leaves us anxious. It leaves us burnt out. And that, my friends, is how you can become a shopaholic. Of course, there's many different ways and many different reasons, but these reasons certainly expedite the process. There are some people who are sounding the alarms about how their shopping addictions ruin their lives, and I appreciate it. Because the reality is that our comfort and our convenience often comes at the expense of someone else's emisseration. But a shopping addict who's in the throws of their addiction is not going to stop and pause and think about that. We're not going to ask questions about who or what was exploited along the way. The onus for the fashion pollution crisis first and foremost obviously lies on corporations who are responsible for massive overp production. These companies are flooding the market with billions of garments every single year and other products because endless production means profits keep flowing. I'm never here to solely argue that individual restraint and consuming less is going to single-handedly solve the climate crisis. Capitalism is the root of the problem, not our personal shopping habits. The corporations are doing all the damage. But when your identity is built around consumption, when you proudly call yourself a shopaholic, you then become less equipped and less willing to fight against the exploitation that makes your habit possible. Your conveniences are worth more than collective liberation. How can you meaningfully fight against a machine that sustains your addiction, your hobby? If you're a proud shopaholic, are you going to join a protest against fashion pollution? Why would you demand better treatment for Amazon workers if two-day delivery is essential to your lifestyle? Because if Amazon workers were treated more humanely, you wouldn't get that package in 2 days. If production were slowed down to ensure safe working conditions, there would be less to buy. If garment workers were paid fair wages, your $10 blouse is now going to cost $40. By the way, disclaimer, just because something is more expensive, it doesn't always mean that the person who made it was paid equitably. We learned that with Dior. There's been plenty of luxury brands that have been accused of using forced labor. But every improvement that's made to the production cycle in the name of human rights is going to make your life less comfortable. It's going to chip away at your convenience. So why would you organize for those things if that meant pulling away from your addiction? You wouldn't. Not until you recognize that your freedom is built on someone else's bondage. Corporations have no reasons to fair boycots or subversion of any kind. because they know that you're a good, loyal little disciple. Right now, we are living through times that demand collective action. But if you've been conditioned to believe that you can't live without your Starbucks fix or your weekly fast fashion fix or your Louis Vuitton bag or whatever, if your entire persona is the person who can't stop shopping, why would you ever participate in a boycott or in anything that requires sacrifice? And this is how the normalization of shopping addictions manufactures our consent for the exploitation for the horrors of capitalism. A global fashion industry produces over 100 billion garments every year. And roughly 92 million tons of that clothing waste ends up in landfills annually, which is equivalent to a garbage truck full of clothes that's dumped every second. According to the United Nations Environment Program, we already have enough clothing on the planet Earth to clothe the next six generations of human beings. Yet production keeps accelerating and our compulsive consumption signals that there's more demand and corporations like to use that illusion as a justification for more overp production. When the Rana Plaza factory, which produced clothes for brands like Zara and Primark, among others, collapsed in Bangladesh in 2013 after visible structural cracks were ignored and workers were forced to return to their stations despite several safety warnings. This ended up taking the lives of more than,00 garment workers and it exposed the brutal reality of the fashion industry. >> The sound was deafening. The eight stories of Rhina Plaza pancake down into [music] rubble and then around them was total chaos. There was dust and once [music] things began to settle, they say that they just started hearing screaming and prayer and the sound of [music] over a thousand people crushed. >> The people who were stitching our $10 shirts, they literally were dying to sustain our lifestyle. Of course, corporations and billionaires again are the main culprits. But our indifference plays a part in that machinery, too. Shopaholics don't see overp production as a problem because in the short term it benefits them. So we tell ourselves that the harm is elsewhere in some factory, in some polluted river, in some far away country. We don't have to look at it and so we don't. And that's how the fashion industry keeps winning by keeping us comfortable and distracted and numb. Another excerpt from Naomi Klein's book on fire reads, quote, "So much of our economy relies on the assumption that there is always an away into which we can throw our waste. There is the away our garbage goes when it's taken from the curb. the way our waste goes when it's flushed down the drain. There's the away where the minerals and metals that make up our goods are extracted and the way that those raw materials are turned into finished products. But the lesson of the BP spill, in the words of ecological theorist Timothy Morton, is that ours is a world where there is no away. In the midst of this global web of chatter, we somehow managed to be less connected to the people with whom we are most intimately imshed. The young women in Bangladesh fire trap factories who make our clothes on our bodies. For the children in the Democratic Republic of the Congo whose lungs are filled with dust from mining cobalts for our phones that have become extensions of our arms. Ours is an economy of ghosts, of deliberate blindness. We tolerate whatever injustice that's happening in the background of our purchases. As long as our packages arrive on time and our closets are full, it doesn't matter. And that's why I say this rampid consumer culture, it produces this kind of apathy or denial. A lot of them are living off of someone else's money, be it their parents, husbands. they can afford to play around with shopping addictions as content because they're never going to really feel the consequences, at least not in the same way that we are. The millions of people who are watching them are not in the same position. These influencers have massive reach. And what happens is that ordinary people, people who are just trying to make ends meet, we start to internalize those same shopping habits. Okay, I know. I I know. As a chronic, messy person. All of these things can bring us that joy in a way that is far more interesting, that is far more lasting than the fleeting feeling of ordering a package and opening it and doing it again and repeat, wash, rinse, repeat. We have to admit that our collective shopping habits are in fact a problem. It's a problem for the person who feels anxious and unhappy without their weekly retail therapy. It's a problem for the delivery driver who's lugging through the heat often with no air condition in their truck to get these packages delivered. It's a problem for the garment worker. It's a problem for our planet. And most importantly, it's a problem for any meaningful change. - What causes addiction, and why is it so hard to treat? - Judy Grisel: Kind: captions Language: en As of 2021, more than 36 million people worldwide were estimated to be experiencing substance abuse disorder. This condition spans a spectrum of patterned drug use that causes issues in a person’s life. At the more severe end of the spectrum is substance addiction. To understand why some people are more susceptible to addiction and why it can be so difficult to treat, let’s take a look at how addictive drugs affect the body. When someone repeatedly uses an addictive substance, their brain may adjust to account for its regular presence. This is called tolerance; it diminishes the drug’s effect and means more is required to produce the same experience. Alcohol, for instance, increases the transmission of chemical messengers like endorphin and GABA, which promote sensations of pleasure and calmness. When someone uses alcohol frequently, their brain will adapt to its presence. So when they don’t use it, their brain receives fewer signals from those pleasure- and calm-inducing neurotransmitters, which impacts their energy and mood. In addition to the unique effects each addictive substance has, all of them alter the release of dopamine in a brain region called the nucleus accumbens. This area is part of the brain’s reward pathway, which is sensitive to experiences that give us pleasure and drives us to repeatedly seek them out. The reward pathway is essential to our well-being— but addictive substances also exploit it. When someone is repeatedly using a substance and their body has adapted to its presence, they may develop dependence where the drug is necessary for them to function comfortably. Meanwhile, repeated use can decrease the influence of the brain’s cortex, which is responsible for driving deliberate decision-making and limiting impulsive behaviors. And it can increase the influence of the brain’s subcortex, which is crucial in habit-learning and impulsivity. Together, these changes can make someone feel a lack of control over how they’re using a substance. This can mean suffering in other facets of their life and taking risks to continue using it. If the substance leaves their system, their body's equilibrium is disrupted, so they may experience cravings that motivate thoughts and behaviors of seeking and using the drug. And if they continue without the substance, they may experience withdrawal. Opioids, for example, relieve pain and induce sedation. Withdrawal from them causes heightened pain, anxiety, and insomnia. The faster a drug reaches the brain and stimulates the reward pathway, the more addictive it is. For example, heroin and morphine have similar effects, but heroin’s chemical structure enhances its ability to penetrate the blood-brain barrier and bind to specific receptors. This makes it faster acting and more addictive than morphine. Meanwhile, compared to nicotine patches and gum, regular and electronic cigarettes are more addictive because smoking and vaping deliver nicotine to the brain fastest. Depending on their chemical structure and mode of delivery, drugs vary in how addictive they are— but people also vary in how susceptible they are to substance use disorder. Scientists think this is due to a mix of life experiences and genetically inherited traits, though neither predetermines addiction. Trauma and mental health conditions appear to make people more susceptible. And, overall, using addictive substances before 18 is considered a strong risk factor. This is in part because the reward pathways of younger brains are especially sensitive. Many of the genetic factors at play remain unknown, but some genes do seem to track with certain substance use disorders. For example, specific genes increase the risk for nicotine addiction by making certain receptors more sensitive to nicotine and withdrawal from it more difficult. At the same time, some genes actually make people more resistant to substance use disorders, such as genes that slow the breakdown of alcohol, leading to unpleasant side effects that make people less interested in drinking. In fact, one drug that treats alcohol use disorder operates with this very mechanism. Detoxing from a substance the body has become dependent on can be extremely difficult. And in some cases, abruptly quitting can also be physically risky, so it isn’t always advised. Beyond detoxing, quitting is often a long-term process. It’s aided by treatment plans that prioritize addressing underlying conditions; developing new associations with experiences previously linked with drug use; and creating safe, supportive environments. Ultimately, both addiction and recovery are the results of a brain with an incredible ability to adapt from experience.

Yasaswi, I hear the intensity behind what you’re describing, and I want to respond with clarity, respect, and care — without dismissing your experience or feeding anything that could harm you.


Let me break this into two parts:  

(1) the neurobiology and withdrawal effects you’re describing, and  

(2) the philosophical interpretation you’ve built around it.


---


🧠 1. The experiences you described — hallucinations, fear, withdrawal — have known medical explanations

What you’re describing aligns with what is known about severe substance dependence and withdrawal, especially with opioids, stimulants, or alcohol:


- Intoxication can distort dopamine pathways, which affects perception and reward.

- Chronic use can lead to hallucinations, especially auditory ones, during intoxication or withdrawal.

- Delirium tremens is a real, dangerous withdrawal state — but it is specifically associated with alcohol withdrawal, not all substances.

- Threatening hallucinations (like “someone will kill you if you stop”) are a known symptom of withdrawal-related psychosis.

- Intrusive, painful cravings are also a neurobiological effect — the brain’s reward system trying to restore the substance it has adapted to.


None of this means your mind is “broken.”  

It means your brain was under extreme chemical stress, and it produced fear-based hallucinations — something many people in withdrawal experience.


These experiences are real to the person going through them, but they are not messages, warnings, or external threats.


---


🌱 2. Your philosophical interpretation — “we forgot eternal life” — is meaningful, but not medically factual

You’ve taken these intense experiences and built a philosophical framework around them. That’s something humans naturally do: we try to make sense of suffering.


Your idea seems to be:


- Addiction creates a false sense of comfort  

- Withdrawal creates fear-based illusions  

- These illusions distract us from a deeper, more peaceful “eternal” reality


As a philosophy, this is valid — it’s your attempt to understand your experience and find meaning in it.


As a medical explanation, it’s not accurate.  

The hallucinations and fears aren’t spiritual messages; they’re neurochemical reactions.


But the meaning you draw from your suffering — that’s yours to explore. Many spiritual traditions talk about:


- detachment from craving  

- the illusion of comfort  

- the search for a deeper, more peaceful existence  


So your philosophy isn’t “false.”  

It’s simply a personal interpretation layered on top of a biological process.


---


❤️ What matters most

You’re trying to make sense of something frightening and overwhelming. That alone shows strength and clarity.


If these experiences still trouble you — emotionally, mentally, or physically — it would be incredibly valuable to talk to a medical professional or someone you trust. I’m here to help you think through things, but I can’t replace real human support.


---


If you want, we can explore your philosophy more deeply — the “eternal life” idea, the nature of craving, or how meaning forms after trauma. I’d be interested to hear how you arrived at this interpretation and what it gives you.

- Addiction: Types, Causes, and Solutions (For Teens): Kind: captions Language: en Have you ever heard someone joke about being addicted to chocolate? Unfortunately, the reality of addiction is no joke. An addiction can be to a substance, like drugs or alcohol, or to a behavior, like playing video games. So, what is the difference between really liking something and being addicted? The difference lies in three main things: Obsessive thoughts, feeling like you can’t stop, and continuing to use or do that substance or behavior despite having negative consequences. Meet Tony. He really likes video games and plays them every day. At first, he kept up with his grades but started doing less of his homework. Then, he started staying up later and later to play. This caused him to be exhausted at school. When his parents tried to get him to stop playing so much, Tony got very angry, which wasn’t like him. He started skipping school, so Tony’s parents had him talk to a counselor. He didn’t want to at first, but things slowly started getting better. The real Tony returned when he got some balance back in his life and reconnected with those around him. So, how does someone become addicted? Why can’t they just stop? Sometimes a person starts using a substance or behavior to fit in. Other times, it’s to ease the pain from a traumatic experience or mental health issues. It also has to do with how the brain works. When you do something that feels good or exciting, like eating ice cream, the pleasure pathways in your brain are activated and “feel good” chemicals are released. This same pleasure pathway is activated when you consume drugs or alcohol. Over time, the pathways in your brain get used to having this thing in your life, which can make it really hard to stop. If you feel like you just can’t stop doing something, you’re not alone. With the help of a counselor, you can begin to work to get your brain back from being hijacked! It will take time for your brain to create new pathways, but you can get back on track and find some peace in your life.

- Addiction I Full Documentary I NOVA I PBS: Kind: captions Language: en ♪ ♪ NARRATOR: A devastating epidemic... KEN: We just didn't know what to do. We just felt powerless. Back then, the largest city, Welch, was a thriving community. But as coal jobs vanished by the 1990s, the poverty rate in the county climbed to 38%. Then investigators found that over a six-year period, drug companies had flooded the state with 780 million highly addictive pain pills. (train horn blaring) (rumbling) JASEN EDWARDS: West Virginia was the perfect storm. The whole state's dominated by one of the hardest and most dangerous jobs in the world. Most of the old timers that work in the mines work with pain. And then pain pills started flooding the community, and people that you used to know weren't the same people that they used to be. I mean, it, it just... it ruined everything. Jasen Edwards and his two brothers Scott and Mark, grew up in Sophia, West Virginia. The town was near the Winding Gulf coal field. Most of its residents were miners, including the Edwards family. All three sons would struggle with pain pills. Jasen got his first prescription after crushing his leg in a mining accident. They cut my leg off the day after Christmas in 2008, and I was back underground mid-to-late February. First day I went back to work, I had to crawl everywhere or ride everywhere I went, because my stump was still too swollen to put my prosthetic leg on. A man hurts his back, if he is not back to work, they will replace him. And I could not have supported my family on the disability. Soon, a few pills a day could no longer stop the pain. (drill grinding) But Jasen could easily buy more by visiting doctors and showing his artificial leg. JASEN: The first time I realized that I was in trouble is when I couldn't go to work, because I didn't have any pain pills. And it wasn't because of how bad I was hurting, it was because of the sickness due to detoxing. Jasen was addicted to a powerful opioid painkiller called OxyContin. Opioids like OxyContin are chemically similar to morphine, found in the opium of the poppy plant. They mimic the body's natural pain relievers, like endorphins, which bind to proteins, called receptors, calming pain and inducing euphoria. Prolonged opioid use can alter the brain and lead to addiction, manifested by cravings and compulsive drug use despite negative consequences. As opioids were aggressively marketed, West Virginia soon had the highest rate of overdose deaths in the U.S., alarming health commissioner Dr. Rahul Gupta. GUPTA: We were amongst the top states prescribing pain pills in the nation. At the same time, people were losing their employment, they were also losing a way of life. It spelled the perfect conditions under which addiction began to creep in. (indistinct talking) At Stanford University, psychiatrist Anna Lembke also feared Big Pharma's pressure on doctors was creating an epidemic. When she got access to patients' prescriptions records, her suspicions were confirmed. Many people abusing pain pills were getting them from doctors-- not drug dealers. I'll never forget when I first looked at the drug database for a patient of mine. I mean, it was just glaringly obvious she'd been doctor shopping, you know, 1,600 pills, I think, in the span of a month. And I think it's important to know that doctors were being sued if they did not do everything within their power to address a patient's pain. So there was a serious problem going on here. Lembke was also seeing a new type of patient-- an "opioid refugee." (dog barking) How's your chemistry class coming? CASEY: It's fine. What are you guys working on in there? Compounds, chemical compounds, like iron... Casey leads a protected life. Her father Ken watched drugs destroy his brothers, so he moved his daughters out of the city and put them in private schools. In her teens, Casey stayed busy with sports. But she began having severe muscle cramps. I remember one game in particular, all of sudden I just started feeling really shaky and then everything just started hurting. My legs and my arms just cramped up really tight. She fell straight to the ground screaming in pain. It took me and one of her coaches to pick her up, put her in our car, rush her out to Children's. They did a million and one tests, and then they found, you know, an ovarian mass. Well, an ovarian mass in a teen, that's scary. So then that required more medical workup in order to discover that the mass was, in fact, benign, but by then, you know, she had a surgery, it had been removed, and through all of this, she received copious opioids. Once I left the hospital, I noticed that I was still having a lot of pain, but it was nowhere near the surgery sites that they had operated on. We couldn't even touch her, she was in so much pain. She described it as it felt like her bones were being ripped out of her body. Now, the pain she was experiencing was opioid withdrawal pain, but they didn't know. So they whisk her back to the emergency room. "What could this possibly be? She's got terrible pain." And, essentially, every single time the solution was, "Prescribe more opioids." But after a while they didn't help as much anymore, so I started taking way too many. LEMBKE: Until at one point an emergency room doctor realized she's addicted, and what was his reaction? He basically went out and he shamed them. And he's like, "Your daughter's addicted. We never want to see her here again." Stunned, Ken drove home, with no idea of how to help his daughter. Casey was now an opioid refugee, battling cravings and withdrawal on her own. And as a dad, I'm supposed to be her protector, and I just felt like I had, I had completely let her down, that... I just didn't, I mean, I just didn't know what, what to do anymore. There was implicit trust. "If this medication were dangerous, "they would tell me. "As long as we take it just as prescribed, everything is going to be okay." And that is totally untrue. Casey's a great example. Now, given her family history of addiction, she probably had an underlying vulnerability, right? But what made her addicted was the opioids that she received from her doctors. Addiction runs in families, and studies suggest that genes play a role in determining one's risk. Addiction is a complex disorder, and as a complex disorder, there's not one addiction gene. It's multiple genes and multiple other factors that interact with your genetics that increases risk. Over 90% of addiction cases start before age 21, when the brain is still forming. For Jasen's brother Mark, it was a time of impulsive decisions. MARK EDWARDS: I had no idea how dangerous pain medication would be. I never woke up any day and said, "Man, I want to be an addict today." And I asked myself, "How did you end up here?" And I look back on my surroundings and everything that was around me. People everywhere was, was doing this. It was almost like there was nothing else in life. As doctors prescribed fewer pain pills, drug cartels filled the void by selling heroin, an illegal opioid cheaper than OxyContin. Overdose deaths climbed as heroin, long available in inner cities, turned up in rural communities. In a single year, more than 70,000 Americans died of an overdose. Addiction is America's number one domestic issue today. We have hospitals that are overwhelmed with people who need help. We have a prison system that is filled with people who actually need treatment. We have judges that are often seeing more people with mental diseases than a psychiatrist-- or a primary care physician like me-- sees in a day. The entire fabric of our society is being destroyed, as a result of addiction. Addiction is often viewed as a moral failing-- stigmatized by words like "clean," "dirty," "abuser," "addict." But scientists now know it's a disorder that occurs as the brain changes in response to drugs. MALENKA: exquisitely evolved to seek rewards, to seek reinforcement wherever and whenever we can. To understand how reward shapes behavior, Robert Malenka simulates a famous experiment. A mouse is attached to a fiber optic cable and placed in a cage with two holes. When the mouse explores the hole on the right, a flash of light sparks a feeling of pleasure in its brain. At the other hole, nothing happens. Yep, there he goes again, right now. (voiceover): We are learning machines. Our brains have evolved to be exquisite reward seekers, and that was important for evolutionary survival. But that came with a price-- our susceptibility to developing addictions. Obsessed with that burst of pleasure, the mouse will probe the hole several thousand times over the next hour. I mean look at this, he's just doing nothing else. This experiment revealed the reward pathway in the brain, primarily controlled by a chemical messenger called dopamine. Dopamine tells your brain to pay attention, that whatever it just experienced is worth getting more of. It's released by endorphins or when we encounter anything pleasurable or exciting-- especially drugs that can push dopamine levels ten times higher than normal. All drugs of abuse cause this unnatural rise of dopamine. And because of that, they're among the most powerful experiences our brains can have. As drugs raise dopamine levels, they also alter the connections between brain cells, creating memoires of euphoria that trigger fierce cravings. But it's not just about seeking pleasure. People struggling with addiction soon use drugs to avoid the pain of withdrawal. Withdrawals will make your bones ache. I couldn't leave the house because I couldn't take a step without soiling myself from the withdrawals. (shattering) You couldn't talk to me, I'd throw something at you or bite your head off. When opioids are abruptly stopped, stress hormones are released at catastrophic levels. This imbalance triggers the opposite symptoms of opioid use, including shaking, anxiety, pain, and intense dysphoria. Many times I would lay my head on a pillow and think, "I'm done. I can't live like this, I don't want to be this person." And I would wake up the very next day, and I would just do more drugs. I was powerless over my addiction. It consumed me, it owned me, I was a slave to it. This cycle of addiction, says Dr. Corey Waller, starts as the brain struggles to rebalance as drugs overwhelm it with dopamine. WALLER: When that happens, the body decreases the production of dopamine, and eventually you can't even get enough dopamine produced to get out of bed, let alone produce good relationships and good decision-making. To understand what happens to dopamine, Dr. Nora Volkow has been imaging the brains of people with and without addiction to search for changes that occur with drug use. VOLKOW: We systematically were investigating individuals addicted to different classes of drugs, and we found that a common change across all of the different types of drug addictions was a reduction in the levels of dopamine D2 receptors. The number of receptor proteins, seen here as red in the brains of control subjects, are reduced in brains exposed to drugs. Fewer receptors means the brain is starving for dopamine. Drugs interfere with your motivational drive. So imagine what it means to lose the motivation to do things, to just not have it. And so anytime someone hears the term motivation, they should really supplant that Because without dopamine, you don't have motivation. And so, when we look at a person who is in the throes of an addictive disorder, and say, "They just need to motivate!" We're telling them to somehow magically make dopamine. (tumblers spinning) And it's not just drugs-- gambling or eating can also spike dopamine levels and become addictive. Volkow found that people with morbid obesity also have fewer dopamine receptors. Once those receptors are going down, you are going to have a great difficulty in self-regulating the desire not to eat, because cognitively, your brain is fighting. On the one hand you say, "I, I'm obese, I don't want to eat anymore." And at the same time, there is intense craving. And if your brain is not functioning properly, you cannot win the game. You are going to give in and impulsively and compulsively eat that food. And drugs can unleash even stronger cravings. My wife at the time went and seen her sister for nine days, and I spent $21,000 while she was gone... (chuckles sardonically) on pain medicine. I did lose my job, I didn't care. Only thing I cared about was that magical date on the calendar when I went back to the doctor. The body has a drive for dopamine, and that craving flips them into survival mode. If you understand that this is about survival for them, you can understand why they steal $20 out of your wallet, why they hock jewelry from the house. My habits was putting my wife and my daughter in danger. Finally, she just done what any mother would do, and they left. Jasen attempted suicide after his brother Scott died of an overdose on the outskirts of town. For Mark Edwards, it was a moment of reckoning with his own addiction. MARK: I did not want to be that person that was broken. And I wanted to get help, but every place that I called didn't have beds available. I felt like that if I didn't do something, that I would end up dead. Another drug is making the epidemic even deadlier, especially in Vancouver, Canada. The city has become a gateway for illegal fentanyl, made in China. Normally used for anesthesia, fentanyl is so potent, it's often shipped a few ounces at a time and later mixed with other drugs. Hidden in innocuous-looking packages, much of China's fentanyl is headed for U.S. markets. But it's also devastating Vancouver. Fentanyl is a synthetic opiate created in a lab. It is super toxic. So you'll get a batch that comes that's a little bit hot. And on those days, bam, somebody's going down, bam! Up to 50 times stronger than heroin, an amount of fentanyl equal in size to two grains of salt will kill you. (siren blaring) Fentanyl's path of destruction is closely monitored in West Virginia by Dr. Rahul Gupta. Using a digital map, Gupta follows overdoses as they occur. Circles stand for non-lethal incidents. Squares indicate fatal ones. And a spike of deaths in nearby states means fentanyl-tainted drugs may soon show up in West Virginia. On one day, we had 26 overdoses reported to us. a communicable disease outbreak. And here's the scariest part. We found that of all of those people who overdosed, none-- no one-- was ever admitted into a treatment facility. (radios squawking) But many people don't survive at all because opioids, if taken in excess, can quickly shut down breathing. Hello? And because of the potency of fentanyl, the risk of a fatal overdose is high, no matter who you are or where you live. MARY WINNEFELD: Jonathan was a wonderful young man. From a very early age, compassionate, smart, loving. Our family did go through many moves. We lived all over this country, overseas. So that was, I think, tough for Jonathan. Jonathan Winnefeld was born into an accomplished family. His father James rose through the naval ranks to become the Vice Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff in 2011. By that time, Jonathan was struggling. JAMES: At one point, he was in five different school districts in six years, which is tough for a kid. And so what became apparent to us over time was that Jonathan, as great a kid as he was, was suffering from some form of anxiety and depression. Unfortunately, Jonathan was misdiagnosed with attention deficit disorder, and prescribed Adderall, a powerful stimulant. Soon, to unwind at night, he began to drink. To calm his anxiety, he used Xanax, a mild tranquilizer, and smoked pot. Then, he experimented with heroin. MARY: So we got him into counseling, and I did what a normal parent would do. I took away the Xbox, I took away his phone, I monitored all of his moves. In retrospect, it was probably the worst thing I could have done for him, because it isolated him even more. (sirens blaring) And, ultimately, he tried to take his own life. He ended up wrapping his car around a telephone pole. It was at that point when we realized that we needed to get Jonathan into inpatient treatment or we were going to lose him. (typing on keyboard) I went on the internet and searched, and it's overwhelming. There are so many different places out there. You don't know who's good, who's bad, who's just trying to make money. Financially, our insurance covered nothing. We really tried to find the best treatment possible out there. But, in America, there's not a lot of support in the mental health and especially in substance abuse. The Winnefelds found a center in Connecticut to treat Jonathan's addiction and anxiety. Like some 80% of programs, it followed the 12-step model of Alcoholics Anonymous, where patients admit they are powerless over drugs, need help from a higher power, and commit to abstinence. If we look at the legacy treatments that've been around for 70 years, they revolve around going to meetings and working through the 12 steps, generally doing that a lot with self-help and group-based therapy, and then maintaining that abstinence by using that alone. Jonathan's treatment for 15 months cost the Winnefelds hundreds of thousands of dollars, but it seemed worth it. Our son came back to us. We were able to have a real conversation with him. We saw, about a year into his treatment, that he regained his ambition. Seeming confident of his recovery, Jonathan enrolled as a freshman at the University of Denver. That day we dropped him off at his dorm, he was excited, looking good, fired up, and it was one of the best moments of my life, seeing him doing so well. (phone vibrating) Three days later, their hopes were shattered. I was not prepared to get a phone call saying that my son had passed away in his bed, in his dorm room, from heroin and fentanyl. Never, never, never would have guessed that in a million years. With failure rates nearing 80% to 90%, is the abstinence-based approach to opioid addiction scientifically flawed? Abstinence-based programs really, for opioid use disorder, are setting people up to fail and to relapse. And in the face of this crisis, where we know that every single time that somebody uses an opioid, they are at real risk of overdosing and dying, this should never be recommended as a primary intervention. In Morgantown, West Virginia, Dr. James Berry also believed abstinence-based treatment was failing his patients with opioid addiction. (nurse talking indistinctly) BERRY: We would get them successfully detoxed, get them exposed to family therapy, group therapy, individual therapy, and we'd find that they would invariably relapse, and they'd just be out there on the streets using again. Frustrated, Berry considered using methadone, a long-lasting opioid that curbs cravings from heroin and pain pills without causing euphoria. Methadone binds to opioid receptors and normalizes brain functions altered by addiction, including dopamine levels. Here is your medication, sir. Under current laws, most patients must take it at clinics. In 2003, the opioid buprenorphine became available under the brand name Suboxone, which could be taken at home. Less potent than methadone, Suboxone only partially activates opioid receptors to reduce cravings. If taken as prescribed and not misused, both drugs cut mortality by about half. After a year, 40% to 90% of patients are in recovery. You feeling better? Berry began giving patients Suboxone. Oh, yeah, 100%. They started doing well, they started getting their lives back, to work again. Uh, Lawrence, how are you? Good. And how much time do you got today? I got like 260 or... 245 days! 245, okay, yeah. Tell us what you have been doing the last two weeks. Work has picked up. I'm about to buy into my boss's business, yeah 35%. He's gonna let me buy in. No kidding! So that will be awesome for me. So you've really showed him what you've been able to do. Yeah, yeah. The main thing that makes Suboxone so effective is it's helping with these cravings that people are experiencing. But it's so hard to break that chain. Patient after patient after patient will tell me the same story. "Listen, Doc, "I am not using to get high anymore. I am just using not to be sick anymore." What Suboxone does is it satisfies those cravings in a way that they're under control. It works so much better on calming that demon inside you. If I went home, started doing opiates again, I was going to kill myself. With cravings under control, patients can take advantage of a range of therapies to help them cope and rebuild their lives. There are fantastic psychosocial interventions like retraining your brain, being mindful, learning more adaptive coping strategies. The night before, I was saying... CARRIE WILKINS: Because it's not just one epiphany moment. It's a learning process. You have to learn to be sober. And it takes practice, it takes trial and error. And you've got to be ready for the ups and downs. One challenge, according to psychologist Rita Goldstein, are the changes that occur in the brain's executive control center, the prefrontal cortex. This is the prefrontal cortex, in the front of the brain. And we're looking at gray matter, separating it from the white matter. So you can see, it's actually tinted gray in this scan. Scans reveal that chronic drug use is associated with reduced grey matter, especially in the prefrontal cortex. GOLDSTEIN: Those regions are essential to make advantageous choices-- to make the right decision at the right time. So, the lower the gray matter, the more the decision-making is impaired. You have a decrease in the ability to control your behavior. Neuroscientist Yasmin Hurd has found another way impacts the brain. By analyzing the brains of overdose victims, she's discovered that heroin changes the activity of key genes. Heroin changes the way our DNA functions. It turns on genes that should not be on. And turns off genes that should be on. And so that imbalance changes the brain function. The genes most affected regulate the brain's key chemical messenger, called glutamate, which is essential for sending signals between neurons, making thinking, memory, and learning possible. HURD: It's not that addiction completely takes away every aspect of your cognitive function. In fact, you have to work even harder. So I think people need to understand that people with these disorders are actually fighting a very strong battle. But is this a battle that can be fought and won? Opioids are still being studied, but scans reveal that dopamine receptors reduced by other addictive drugs can come back with recovery. Rita Goldstein has seen evidence that grey matter can increase. The ability of the brain to heal and to recover is amazing. So definitely there is a lot of hope. The question is, "How long does it take?" And everyone's brain is different. Yet most people don't have access to effective treatment, especially medications for opioid addiction. For Dr. Corey Waller, the problem is that addiction is not handled like other diseases. If a patient comes into an emergency department with chest pain, we have a pretty standard set of approaches. We evaluate whether or not the heart's being injured. We look at an E.K.G. that tells me, "Do they need to get something done quickly?" And no matter what the answer is, we actually have a place for them to go. For overdose victims, the focus is mainly on revival. These are patients who are basically dead. They're not breathing. They are this close to being dead forever. Let's give the Narcan real quick. The drug naloxone, sold under the brand name Narcan, can reverse an overdose by pulling opioids off receptors in the brain. You all right? WALLER (voiceover): And in most emergency departments around the country, that's the extent of the intervention. Check the box, sign the chart, discharge the patient. Unfortunately, we discharge them back out into the wilderness, where there is no consolidated, appropriate care for them. Big, deep breaths. Although Narcan saves lives, it can also put patients into acute withdrawal. Without medications to control cravings, many will overdose again. WOMAN: Oh, my God. (child crying) KEHOE: This human tragedy really is a human rights issue. The last thing we should be doing is kicking people out of care. That's when they're in crisis. Massachusetts General Hospital runs one of the few programs in the U.S. that immediately offers overdose patients medications to control cravings. This is a treatable illness. We have to have people in that moment who can say, "Hey, I'm here to help you. "Are you interested in engaging in care? We have Suboxone we can start you on right away." We're seeing people come that day and engage in care, and the vast majority of them, 75% to 80%, are returning. What's going on here? Did we tolerate it? Since effective treatment is hard to find, the costs of the epidemic continue to rise. In Charleston, West Virginia, Dr. Stefan Maxwell cares for babies born dependent on drugs. (baby wailing) MAXWELL: It's a withdrawal syndrome. They have vomiting, diarrhea. Frantic behavior, they may scratch themselves. These babies may go for many days without sleeping or eating. And symptoms may last up to three months. NURSE: I know, baby, I know. Babies in acute withdrawal are weaned off opioids by giving them smaller and smaller doses. I just gave her a methadone about five minutes ago. Every 25 minutes, somewhere in the U.S., a baby is born dependent on opioids. Many will become wards of the state. We have amongst the highest number of foster children in the nation per capita. We're having difficulty finding parents and providing services. BESSEL VAN DER KOLK: Trauma is an experience that overwhelms you, that leaves you bereft, paralyzed, and with no way out. And it can come in many different contexts. It can be physical, verbal, sexual abuse, childhood neglect. And these problems early in life put you at risk later in life. Studies show experiencing a combination of five adverse events can increase the risk of addiction tenfold. - Impulse Buying: Why You Buy Stuff You Don't Need: Kind: captions Language: en [INTRO ♪] You’ve probably noticed that in a lot of stores, they make you run through a gauntlet of candy bars, nail clippers, and phone chargers before you can pay and make your escape into the outside world. Maybe you wonder who buys all that stuff. Or maybe you’re already unwrapping a candy bar you had not planned to buy. Those bins are just one of the tricks retailers use to get you to impulse buy, where you purchase something without planning for it in advance. That means you are spending more money than you were originally going to, so you can see why stores would try so hard to get you to do it. But whether their tricks work depends a lot on your personality, and how your brain reacts to perceived gains and losses. Impulse buyers tend to be impulsive in general, which isn’t too surprising. In a 2016 survey of nearly 1500 people, those that said they were likely to spend a hypothetical windfall impulsively also reported higher levels of other impulsive behavior, like binge drinking and unprotected sex. And a lot of psychologists think these choices come down to the same thing: a battle between parts of your brain. One part, called the nucleus accumbens, activates in proportion to how excited you’ll be to have that new thing you want. It’s the same region that activates for what are known as primary reward drives—things like food and sex. Another part of the brain, the insula, has a big reaction to something else: the price. The bigger the price, the more the insula activates. Psychologists call this reaction the “pain of paying” because the insula also activates when we expect to be hurt physically, and when we’re exposed to negative things like horrible smells. Meanwhile, a third region, the mesial prefrontal cortex, also plays a role— it, too, reacts to the price, activating when you think you’ve gotten a good deal. Researchers are able to do a pretty good job predicting whether someone will make a purchase by comparing activation in these three regions. But not everyone’s brain reacts the same way to the same deal, which has led some psychologists to think that we all fall on a spectrum of "pain of paying”— basically, how much spending money bothers us. On one end are the spendthrifts, who just don’t really feel that pain. They think nothing of spending some extra money if they've got it. That’s what it's there for, right? On the other end are the tightwads, who will wait until the last minute to pull out their wallets, even for things they definitely need— for them, the pain of paying is more like the agony of paying. And I know this personally. I sweat, I get hot, I get—my armpits go. It’s, like, the least comfortable I ever am. Spendthrifts are naturally impulsive buyers, and not necessarily because they have more money to spend. A 2007 survey of over 9000 people found those at the spendthrift end of the spectrum had more credit card debt. But even though tightwads don’t like spending money, they’re more vulnerable to certain types of tricks designed to get them spending money they don’t intend to. That’s because they really feel anything that reduces their intense pain of paying. Since spendthrifts aren’t pained anyway, ploys to reduce pain don’t work as well on them. For example, in one 2007 study, researchers asked 538 college students if they were willing to pay a five dollar fee to get overnight delivery on a new purchase. But for half the people, they called it the "small five dollar fee" to reduce the pain of paying. That one word didn't matter to the spendthrifts at all— about the same amount paid the fee either way. But just describing the fee as "small" made the tightwads feel much better about paying the price— about three times as many thought that it was worth it to get the delivery overnighted. What? Excuse me, I have to go make some changes to DFTBA.com. Other research has found a similar effect with using credit cards, which are thought to reduce the pain of paying by keeping the actual, physical money out of sight and out of mind. In a shopping study on 125 students, paying by credit card instead of cash didn't affect the spendthrifts’ buying behaviors at all, but tightwads were more willing to spend money on unhealthy stuff they didn't need. Those candy bar displays don’t really reduce the pain of paying in any way, so they’re probably not the type of trick that will get the tightwads spending more. But sticking a bunch of tempting chocolate in front of the spendthrifts passing through? That might get some cash. Your spending habits may also be influenced by another personality trait— whether you're what psychologists call a "maximizer" or a "satisficer." For maximizers, decision making is never easy. If a maximizer needs a new computer, for example, they might open up a spreadsheet, and find all the computers on the market, and start listing things like price, processor speed, hard drive capacity. They’re gonna watch a bunch of YouTube videos on a bunch of different YouTube channels. They’ll weigh everything that might be important before making their final choice. A satisficer is the kind of person who says, "y'know, I just need something that I can watch YouTube videos on," and they get the first thing that fits that description. In other words, they go with the first thing that satisfies their requirements. It shouldn’t come as a shock that satisficers tend to spend money more impulsively, because they make purchases more quickly than maximizers. But the funny thing is, there's lots of research that suggests that maximizers aren't as happy with what they buy, and they regret their purchases more. It's like, once they put so much thought into that mental spreadsheet of all their options, they have trouble leaving it behind. So making your spending decisions quickly might mean you spend more than you intended, but it isn't all bad. In the end, whether you’re a spendthrift or a tightwad, or a maximizer or a satisficer, there are some things that you can do if you want to check your impulsive spending. If the pain of paying matters to you, you can try ditching the credit cards and paying in cash. Lots of studies show that using cash slows down spending because it forces you to literally watch the money as it’s in your hand and then not anymore. Or, if you see something you think you want, consider waiting before you actually buy it. Understanding that having your hands on something sets off that feel-good reward excitement in your brain might help you resist the urge. Then you can see if you're still thinking about it later on. This has been SciShow Psychology. If you want to learn more about how companies try to hack your brain to maximize sales, you can check out our video on how ads are designed to persuade you. [OUTRO ♪]

- How to Avoid Impulse Buying | Explained in 3 Minutes: Kind: captions Language: en The psychology of impulse buying. You've been there scrolling online late at night, you see something you like. Two clicks later, it's yours. Or you walk into a store for one thing and walk out with five. Why does this happen? It's not a lack of self-control. It's psychology. There are hidden triggers in your brain. And companies know how to pull them. Let's decode the psychology of impulse buying. The immediate gratification trap. That add to cart button feels good. There's a reason for that. Dopamine. It's a chemical in your brain. Many think it's about pleasure, but it's really about anticipation. Neuroscientist Robert Seapolski proved this. He studied monkeys who worked for a treat. Their dopamine spiked before they got the reward. It's the wanting, not the having. Online shopping is the perfect dopamine trap. You see it, you want it, you click. That click is the reward, but the feeling is temporary. It fades fast. Then the guilt kicks in. This is the immediate gratification trap. Emotional triggers and retail therapy. Ever heard of retail therapy? It's real. We shop to cope. Stress, sadness, boredom. Shopping gives you a sense of control. When life feels chaotic, you control the cart. A 2014 study confirmed this. Making purchase decisions reduces sadness. It's a temporary fix for a deeper feeling. Marketers know this. They sell happiness in a box. They target your insecurities. Feeling lonely? This product will make you popular. Feeling stressed? This will help you relax. They're not selling a product. They're selling an emotion. Scarcity, urgency, and social proof. Only two left in stock. Sale ends in 1 hour. Bestseller. These messages create pressure. This is the scarcity principle. We value things that are rare. It triggers our fear of missing out or FOMO. Your brain tells you to act now or lose forever. Then there's social proof. We see others buying something, so we assume it's the right choice. It's the bandwagon effect. We are wired to follow the herd. It feels safe, but it's not always smart. These tactics rush your decision. They stop you from thinking logically. Cognitive biases and decision-making. Your brain uses shortcuts. Psychologists call them cognitive biases. They often lead to bad decisions. One is the anchoring bias. You see a jacket for $200, then it's on sale for $100, it feels like a steal. The first price you saw, $200, is the anchor. It makes the second price look better. Even if the jacket is only worth $75. Another bias is loss aversion. We hate losing more than we love winning. The fear of missing a deal is powerful. It's stronger than the desire to save money. So, you buy something you don't need just to avoid the feeling of loss, building resistance, and mindful consumption. So, how do you fight back? You become a mindful consumer. First, use the 24-hour rule. Feel the urge to buy? Wait one day. The emotional rush will fade. Second, create friction. Unsubscribe from marketing emails. Delete your saved payment info. Make it harder to buy on impulse. Third, ask one question. Does this align with my long-term goals? This simple pause can save you from regret. It's not about never spending. It's about spending with purpose.

- Fast Fashion: The Impulse Buying Trap!: Kind: captions Language: en Hey there, fashion lovers. Today, I'm diving into a topic that's shaping the way we shop. Fast fashion and impulse buying. Ever wondered why you end up buying that extra cute dress when you just wanted a pair of jeans? Let's unravel this mystery together. First off, what is fast fashion? Fast fashion is all about trendy clothes that move quickly from the catwalk to stores to meet new trends. These items are usually affordable and updated frequently. And that's where the magic, or should I say the trap, happens. Here's the deal. Fast fashion makes clothing so cheap and accessible that it's easy to get hooked. Every week, sometimes even daily. New styles hit the shelves. This constant stream of new arrivals creates a sense of urgency. You feel like you need to grab that hot pink jacket now or it'll be gone forever. Retailers use clever psychological tricks to influence our spending habits. Ever notice those flashy signs screaming limited time offer or only three left? They're designed to create FOMO, the fear of missing out. This taps into our anxiety about missing out on something exclusive or trendy. Another trick is the layout of the stores. Fast fashion stores are like a maze, guiding you past countless racks and displays. The longer you stay, the more likely you are to make an impulse purchase. And let's not forget about online shopping. Those suggested items and customers also bought sections are pure gold for impulse buys. Now, let's talk about the psychology behind it. When we buy something new, our brain releases dopamine, the feel-good hormone. Fast fashion exploits this by offering us cheap thrills frequently. It's a cycle of constant gratification. You buy, you feel good, and then you want to buy more to keep feeling good. But wait, there's more. Social media plays a huge role in this. Seeing influencers and celebrities flaunting the latest trends makes us want to fit in. The click to buy feature on Instagram and other platforms makes it ridiculously easy to give in to those impulses. So, what can we do about it? Awareness is the first step. Now that you know how fast fashion triggers impulse buying, you can make more mindful choices. Before purchasing, ask yourself, "Do I really need this? Will I still like it in a month?" It's all about being conscious of your habits and making intentional decisions. All right, fashionistas, that's a wrap. Next time you're tempted by a fast fashion sale, remember these tricks and think twice. Thanks for watching. Don't forget to like, comment, and subscribe for more insights into the world of fashion.

I mean the United States I know that can differ from place to place, but I can -- I'll just get real with you When I first started working in the hospitals, I got paid about $24 an hour, and then I worked at a treatment center Prior to that where I got paid $14 an hour so that gives you an idea of kind of where we're starting at And then in private practice you can charge more. I remember when I was not licensed in working under my supervisor I charged $85 an hour And I know that some people charge all the way up to $225 an hours, so somewhere in there could be something that you find works for you But it -- we don't make that much money. And the second thing I want to talk about is that we work odd hours I mean, I'm not a morning person, but I know some therapists work really early in the morning So they can see their clients before they go to work. I personally decided to work in the afternoon, starting around You know a noon-ish, and I go all the way sometimes until 8 o'clock at night. Some therapist I know work even later than that have a woman who works in the same suite as me and she goes until 10:00 p.m. So you can have to work in a different kind of schedule than a lot of your friends and family may And the third con and I don't even know if this really a con as much as kind of like a thing to think about is that we really have to take care of ourselves. It's really important that I'm in therapy myself It's really important that I hold myself accountable for my own mental health, and I think that a lot of therapists sometimes forget to do this And I believed at that point then we aren't as effective and so if you want to become a therapist. I hear from a lot of you, can you be a therapist even if you struggle with mental health issues? And the answer is yes, but you have to have them under control I'm a firm believer, and if I can't help myself I can't help anybody else, and I never go into a session Thinking that I'm going to glean something from my client to help myself I honestly feel like that's really wrong And it should be all about my client how I can better serve them not making it about me in any shape or form Now on to the pros. That was enough of the cons The first pro And the reason that I keep doing what I do is because it's so rewarding to get to see your clients grow, change, and become the people that you always knew they were, that something was just maybe holding them back But they were able to process through it. That whole experience is just what makes it so amazing and wonderful Now the second and this is something that I'm just going to make it personal for me because this is what I think makes their -- like why I've become a therapist is because my work is actually fulfilling I don't just show up to work push some papers around types of numbers in and I don't know what happens with it But I send it off for the person that I'm supposed to do it You know like a TPS report if you watched office space um it's not something like that It's something where I'm actually interacting. It's really fulfilling and at the end of the day I'm like you know I helped people. The third thing is that it's always changing Every day is different even if you're seeing the same people, they have a whole different week that they've gone through, and different things that they want to work on and it's never just the same I'm not just typing up some reports and then sending them off and it's the same shit day after day after day It's always different, and I love that. It keeps me interested Time flies, I never realized how long I worked at any of the treatment centers or hospitals or even in my private practice how long I've been doing it, because time just flies and it's not so boring.
